Greek (Kühn)

διαφέρει γὰρ τὸ τιμωρῶν τοῦ τιμωρούμενος, ὃ μετὰ τῆς 385 καλουμένης αἰτιατικῆς πτώσεως σημαίνει τὸ κολάζον· οὕτως γοῦν καὶ ἑαυτὸν τιμωρούμενος ἐπιγέγραπται τὸ Μενάνδρου δρᾶμα. Μίαν μὲν ἐν τῷ ἔμπροσθεν μεσηγὺ τῆς κεφαλῆς τοῦ βραχίονος καὶ τοῦ τένοντος τοῦ κατὰ τὴν μασχάλην. Ἐν τούτῳ τῷ χωρίῳ καὶ ἡμῖν ἑώραται τὸ ἄρθρον ἐστηριγμένον, ὅπερ ἐστὶ μόριον ἁπάσης τῆς ἔμπροσθεν χώρας· οὔσης γὰρ ὅλης αὐτῆς πλατείας ἓν μέν τι μέρος ἐστὶν ἀκριβῶς μέσον, ἄλλα δὲ ἑκατέρωθεν δύο, τὸ μὲν ἐκ τῶν ἔνδοθεν μερῶν, οὗ νῦν ἐμνημόνευσε, τὸ δὲ ἐκ τῶν ἐκτὸς, οὗ μετὰ ταῦτα μνημονεύει. καὶ θαυμάσαι γε ἔστιν αὐτὸν ὅτι μηδ’ ὅλως ἑωρακὼς εἰς ἄλλο χωρίον ἐκπῖπτον τὸ ἄρθρον ἢ κατὰ τὴν μασχάλην οὐκ ἤρκεσε τῇ κατ’ αὐτὸν καύσει μόνῃ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For τιμωρῶν differs from τιμωρούμενος, which, with the 385 so-called accusative case, signifies 'punishing'; thus at any rate the play of Menander is entitled Heauton timoroumenos (The Self-Punisher). One in front, between the head of the humerus and the tendon at the armpit. In this place the joint has been seen by us too to be lodged, which is a part of the whole anterior region; for since the whole of it is broad, one part is exactly in the middle, and two others lie on either side, the one on the inner parts, which he has mentioned now, the other on the outer, which he mentions after this. And one may indeed admire him in that, although he had never at all seen the joint dislocated into any other place than at the armpit, he was not content with the cautery at that place alone.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τῇ κατ’ αὐτὸν καύσει (masculine αὐτὸν has no clear referent; taken loosely as 'at that site', i.e. the armpit), τὸ κολάζον (rendered 'the one who punishes'; the sense may rather be 'punishing')

For τιμωρῶν differs from τιμωρούμενος, which, with the so-called 385 accusative case, signifies the one who punishes; thus, at any rate, Menander's play is entitled 'Heauton timoroumenos' (punishing himself). 'One in front, between the head of the upper arm and the tendon at the armpit.' In this region we too have seen the joint lodged. It is a part of the whole anterior region: since the whole of that region is broad, one part of it lies exactly in the middle, and two others lie on either side of it, one on the inner side, which he has now mentioned, and the other on the outer side, which he mentions afterwards. And one may well wonder at him that, although he had never at all seen the joint falling out into any other region than into the armpit, he was not content with the cauterization at that site alone.

προστιθεὶς δὲ δύο ἑτέρας ἐκείνων ἀκριβῶς ἐστοχάσατο τῶν χωρίων, ἐν οἷς καὶ ἡμεῖς εἴδομεν ἐστηριγμένην τὴν κεφαλὴν 386 τοῦ βραχίονος. ἐν οἷς τῆς ὠμιαίας φλεβὸς χώρα, καθ’ ἣν οἱ δύο μύες συμβάλλουσιν, ὅ τε ἀπὸ τοῦ στέρνου μέγας καὶ ὁ δελτοειδὴς ὀνομαζόμενος ἑώραται τετράκις ἡμῖν ὑποδεξαμένη τὴν ἔκπτωσιν. ἡ δ’ ἔξωθεν τῆς μέσης χώρας ἅπαξ, ὑπὲρ ἧς ἐφεξῆς ἐρεῖ. Καὶ ταύτη τὸ μὲν δέρμα τελέως διακαίειν χρὴ, βαθύτερον δὲ οὐ χρή. φλέψ τε γὰρ παχείη πλησίη καὶ νεῦρα, ὧν οὐδέτερα θερμαντέα. Τὴν προσαγορευομένην ὠμιαίαν φλέβα λέγει νῦν παχεῖαν ἐν ἴσῳ τῷ εὐρεῖαν ἢ μεγάλην. ἐπίκειται δὲ αὐτῇ συμβολὴ τῶν προειρημένων δύο μυῶν ἔχουσα παρακείμενον νεῦρον, ἀφ’ οὖ πᾶν τὸ πέριξ τῆς φλεβὸς δέρμα ἀπονεμήσεις λαμβάνει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀπονεμήσεις

But by adding two others he hit precisely upon those regions in which we too have seen the head 386 of the humerus lodged. Among these, the region of the shoulder vein (ὠμιαία φλέψ), where the two muscles come together, the great one from the sternum and the one named deltoid, has been seen by us four times to receive the dislocation; and the region outside the middle one, once, about which he will speak next. And here the skin must be burnt through completely, but one must not go deeper; for a thick vein is near, and nerves, neither of which is to be heated. He now calls the vein termed the shoulder vein 'thick', in the same sense as 'wide' or 'large'. Upon it lies the junction of the two muscles mentioned before, having a nerve lying alongside it, from which all the skin around the vein receives its distributions (ἀπονεμήσεις).

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀφ’ οὖ (read as ἀφ’ οὗ, 'from which'), ἀπονεμήσεις

But in adding two others besides those, he aimed accurately at the places in which we too have seen the head 386 of the upper arm lodged. Among these, the region of the humeral vein, where the two muscles meet, both the large one from the sternum and the one called deltoid, has been seen by us four times to have received the dislocation; the region outside the middle one, once, and about this he will speak next. "Here too one must burn completely through the skin, but must not go deeper; for a thick vein lies near, and nerves, neither of which is to be heated." He now calls the so-called humeral vein "thick", as equivalent to "wide" or "large". Over it lies the junction of the two muscles mentioned before, which has a nerve lying beside it, from which all the skin around the vein receives branches.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: διακαύσουσα, κατὰ κύκλου, διαρθρώσεως, πρώτοις, νεύρων

That one must take care not to touch the nerve and the vein in the cautery of the skin he has taught clearly 387; he has not, however, made plain whether he orders one to burn in the first parts of the vein, or in the inner parts, or exactly along it. For it is possible to do this by first stretching up the skin in the manner that has been described in the case of the armpit, then burning through transversely (διακαύσουσα, perhaps for διακαύσαντα), and then passing the spatula through and making the second pressing-on of the cautery in a circle (κατὰ κύκλου); for just as in the armpit, although the largest nerves lie beneath, and along with them an artery and a very wide vein, he nevertheless ordered the skin to be burned, providing safety for the underlying parts by the fitting-through (διαρθρώσεως) of the spatula, so too in the case of the cautery just spoken of it is nothing remarkable that he should think it right to place the eschars upon the very skin that lies over the vein.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πρώτοις (perhaps corrupt, e.g. for πρόσω: 'front parts'?), διακαύσουσα (feminine participle, grammatically anomalous; read as διακαύσαντα), κατὰ κύκλου (perhaps for κατὰ κύκλον), ὑπάλειπτρον / ὑπολείπτρου (spatula; spelling varies), διαρθρώσεως (odd in the sense of the spatula's 'interposition'; possibly corrupt)

That one must take care not to touch either the nerve or the vein in the cauterization of the skin, he has 387 taught clearly. He did not, however, make plain whether he bids one burn at the first parts of the vein, or at the inner parts, or exactly over it. For it is possible to do this by first drawing up the skin in the manner described for the armpit, then burning it through transversely, and then passing the spatula through and making the second application of the cautery round about. For just as in the armpit, although very large nerves lie beneath, and with them an artery and a very wide vein, he nevertheless ordered the skin to be burnt, providing safety for the underlying parts by the interposition of the spatula, so also in the cauterization just described it is nothing surprising that he should think fit to place the eschars on the very skin that lies over the vein.

Ὄπισθεν δ’ αὖ ἄλλην ἐσχάρην ἐνδέχεται ἐνθεῖναι, ἀνωτέρω μὲν συχνῷ τοῦ τένοντος τοῦ κατὰ τὴν μασχάλην, κατωτέρω δὲ ὀλίγῳ τῆς κεφαλῆς τοῦ βραχίονος, καὶ τὸ μὲν δέρμα τελέως χρὴ διακαίειν, βαθεῖαν δὲ μηδὲ κάρτα ταύτην ποιέειν· πολέμιον γὰρ τὸ πῦρ νεύροισιν. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: παρὰ δὲ τοῦ τῆς ὠμιαίας φλεβὸς

Those nerves, then, he called 'most critical cords', being accustomed to use this name for those which possess great power, as those do, in virtue both of being near the spinal marrow and of being themselves very thick in substance; but the one alongside the shoulder vein he called simply 'nerve', 388 no longer adding 'critical'. For it is both very far from the spinal marrow and small in size. "Behind, again, it is possible to place another eschar, considerably higher than the tendon at the armpit, but a little lower than the head of the humerus; and the skin must be burned through completely, but this one should not be made very deep; for fire is hostile to nerves." He has arrived in his account at the remaining region among those that receive the joint at the shoulder when it dislocates; this is in the outer parts of the forward region, into which region too the humerus has been seen by me once to have been dislocated. But in most of the copies the wording is not correctly written thus, 'and again another posterior eschar'; for this place is not behind the articulation, but on the outside.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: παρὰ δὲ τοῦ τῆς ὠμιαίας φλεβὸς νεῦρον (construction apparently corrupt; perhaps τὸ δὲ παρὰ τῇ ὠμιαίᾳ φλεβὶ νεῦρον; rendered 'the nerve beside the shoulder vein'), τόνους (rendered 'cords'), Ὄπισθεν (the lemma's reading 'Behind' seems to conflict with Galen's objection to the variant ὀπισθίην; the text may be corrupt), ὀπισθίην

For it is both farthest from the spinal marrow and small in size. 'Behind, again, it is possible to place another eschar, a good deal higher than the tendon at the armpit, but a little lower than the head of the upper arm; and one must burn through the skin completely, but not make this one very deep either; for fire is hostile to nerves.' He has come in his account to the remaining one of the regions that receive the shoulder joint when it falls out. This lies in the outer parts of the front region, into which too I have once seen the upper arm dislocated. In most of the copies the text is incorrectly written thus: 'and again another posterior eschar'; for this place is not behind the articulation but on the outside.

If, at any rate, someone wished accurately to delimit the regions lying around the articulation at the shoulder, 389 which have breadth, it is clear that, conceiving all of them, in the front parts there is in the middle a certain line without breadth, conceived as precisely the middle, stretched from above along the length of the upper arm, while in breadth on either side the front is bounded: on the inner region by the shoulder vein, and on the outer side, again, up to the extent of another, equal interval, namely that by which this vein stands apart from the breadthless line. These, then, are the boundaries of the front region; and on either side of the two there are others, from which (ἀφ’ ἦς) the tendons at the armpit [take their limit], the one bounding the inner region, the other the outer.

These, then, are the boundaries of the front region; and on either side of these two there are others, from which (?) the tendons at the armpit, the one bounding the inner region, the other the outer.

Let the inner region of the articulation be understood by you as belonging to the tendon which, together with the vein, and not between [them], circumscribes the axilla from the outside; and further to that line, of those intervals from which I required a certain interval to lie between the shoulder vein and the exactly anterior middle line, so that according to this division, insofar as it is higher than the outer tendon at the axilla, 390 the hollow of the scapula would not be behind the articulation, just as indeed the axilla is the region at the shoulder, while the upper region is between the acromion and the head of the humerus. One must therefore treat the wounds throughout the whole course of the treatment, never stretching the upper arm upward strongly, but moderately, only so far as is needed for the care of the wounds; for thus it would be less chilled. Ὅταν δὲ δὴ καθαρὰ γένηται τὰ ἕλκεα ἐς ὠτειλάς τε ἴῃ, τότε δὴ καὶ παντάπασι χρὴ αἰεὶ τὸν βραχίονα πρὸς τῇσι πλευρῇσι προδεδέσθαι καὶ νύκτα καὶ ἡμέρην.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐκπλήσσοιτο, τουτέστιν ἐπὶ πλέον διΐστασθαι τὸ τοῖς χείλεσιν ἕλκος, οὐ τοῦ σπασμοῦ κίνδυνος ἀκολουθήσειε μόνος, προδεδέσθαι

He himself stated the causes on account of which he orders that the upper arm never be stretched up strongly: first, that it is beneficial to cover all burns, so that they may be treated and not chilled; for in general too, as he himself said, for wounds the 391 cold is biting, hardens the skin all around, and produces pain that does not come to suppuration. And much more so in the case of those wounds which a hot cause has produced, as here the fire. "And it would be less struck apart," he says, that is, the wound's being made to gape more widely at its lips; for in this condition it is chilled more, takes longer to cicatrize, and has the scars themselves larger. What is said next, "and it would be less liable to hemorrhage," he added having been carried over into the general account concerning all wounds, not as something peculiar to those now before us; for if one were to burn through the skin in the way he ordered, no fear of hemorrhage would follow. But perhaps, from the sinews being chilled once the skin has been laid bare, the danger of spasm would follow, and not that alone; and this too he mentioned at the end of the whole passage. But when the wounds have become clean and are proceeding to scars, then indeed the upper arm must be bound beforehand to the ribs entirely and always, both night and day.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐκπλήσσοιτο, τουτέστιν ἐπὶ πλέον διΐστασθαι τὸ τοῖς χείλεσιν ἕλκος, οὐ τοῦ σπασμοῦ κίνδυνος ἀκολουθήσειε μόνος, γυμνωθέντος τοῦ δέρματος, νεῦρα

He himself stated the reasons for which he orders that the upper arm never be raised forcibly. The first is that it is beneficial to keep all burns covered, so that they may be healed and not chilled. For in other cases too, as he himself used to say, cold is biting to wounds, hardens the skin round about, and produces pain that does not come to suppuration; and this holds much more for those which a hot cause has produced, as here the fire. "And it would be less," he says, "ἐκπλήσσοιτο": that is, that the wound be drawn further apart at its lips. For in this condition it is chilled more, cicatrises more slowly, and has larger scars themselves. What is said next, "and it would bleed less," he added because he was carried along into the general account of all wounds; it is not peculiar to those now under discussion. For if someone burned through the skin in the way he ordered, no fear of haemorrhage would follow. But perhaps, from the chilling of the nerves when the skin has been stripped off, it is not the danger of spasm alone that would follow; and he mentioned this too at the end of the whole passage. "But when the wounds have become clean and are proceeding to cicatrices, then indeed the upper arm must always and altogether be kept bound to the sides, both night and day."

392 Here he used "ὠτειλαί" in its proper sense, of scars, not, as before, of wounds. He requires one to take especial care at this time that the upper arm should never be moved away from the ribs, except only for a short while at that moment when the wounds are being treated. For the clean wounds appear to be especially seized by all the symptoms which he mentioned in the passage preceding this one, and above all by those arising from external causes. Since previously, when they still came to us (ἥκειν) dirty and inflamed, their filth served them as a kind of bulwark and a kind of covering, as did the superabundance of the humours contrary to nature; and moreover the heat of the inflammation held out against the cold things that befall them (μὴ γινομένοις) from outside. But when it is inflamed (φλεγμαίνει), then, having become clean and, as it were, free of superfluity, it perceives more than before what it suffers. For all these reasons it is both easily pained and chilled by external causes, and especially by cold ones; and through the acuteness of its sensation it has the pain more violent and acquires in addition the troubles that attend upon it.

Here too he has himself made plain the aim of what he advised, by saying: "for in this way above all would it scar over and the open space be done away with, along which the upper arm slips out", saying in effect this: the shorter the scar becomes, the more will the skin around the armpit be drawn together into a small compass; and this is of the greatest importance for keeping the upper arm from readily slipping out at that place.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ταὐτὸν, διά τε τὴν διαστροφὴν ... αἰτία δὲ

In those in whom a shoulder has been despaired of as regards reduction, if they are still in the period of growth, the bone of the 394 humerus is not willing to grow along with the rest in the same way as the healthy one, but it does grow to some extent, yet becomes shorter than the other. He now goes through the symptoms which of necessity supervene in those who have the shoulder dislocated. The first, then, and the most common, upon which all the others also follow, is that the same limb becomes more atrophic; and upon this in turn there follows, when the limb is already fully grown, thinness, and when it is still growing, smallness as well. This atrophy comes about both through the distortion of the vessels and muscles in the limb, and the cause is the immobility of the whole joint; for indeed any one of the healthy limbs, if it has remained wholly unmoved for a long time, plainly appears more atrophic.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καταπορηθῇ, τὸ ἀτροφώτερον ταὐτὸν γίγνεσθαι, τούτῳ δ’ αὐτῷ πάλιν ἕπεται. τελειωθέντος, διά τε τὴν διαστροφὴν

In those in whom the shoulder cannot be put back in, if they are still growing, the bone of the 394 upper arm will not grow together with the rest in the same way as the healthy one; it does grow to some extent, but it becomes shorter than the other. He now goes through the symptoms that necessarily supervene in those who have the shoulder dislocated. The first, then, and the most common, on which all the others follow, is that this same part becomes less well nourished; and on this in turn there follows, when the limb has already reached its full growth, thinness, and, when it is still growing, also smallness. This atrophy comes about through the distortion of the vessels and muscles in the limb, and its cause is the immobility of the whole joint; for indeed any one of the healthy limbs, if it has remained entirely immobile for a long time, plainly appears less well nourished.

And those who are called galiankones from birth come to be so through these two misfortunes: if indeed some such dislocation befalls them while they are in the womb, and through another misfortune, about which something will be written at a later point. Whether the galiankones were so named from their resemblance to weasels (γαλαῖ) or from something else, one must leave it to those who busy themselves with such matters to investigate carefully. For the physician, however, it is useful to know that some are born with both the joints at the shoulder dislocated, the left and the right, and that these of necessity have small upper arms. One must also know that there is some other misfortune that produces the same affection, about which he announces that he will speak again. But it is difficult to find any other than the one which he himself says next befalls infants. The text runs as follows: Moreover, those in whom, while they are still infants, deep and submerged (ὑποβρύχιοι) suppurations occur at the head of the humerus—these too all become galiankones.

and whether they are incised 396 or cauterized or it bursts of its own accord for them, one must know well that these things are so. For it is possible for suppuration to take place, for it to burst and to scar over, in the infant still in the womb, through the agency of nature; and this is admissible still more in the case of a dislocation (ἔμπτωσιν) of the joint at the shoulder, both on account of the moistness of the child's nature and on account of the ease with which the socket at the articulation is overstepped.

397 Οἷσι δ’ ἂν ἤδη ἂν ἀνδράσιν ἐοῦσι ἐκπέσῃ ὁ ὦμος καὶ μὴ ἐμβληθῇ, ἐπωμὶς ἀσαρκωτέρη γίνεται καὶ ἡ ἕξις λεπτὴ ἡ κατὰ τοῦτο, ὅταν μέντοι ὀδυνώμενοι παύσωνται. ὁκόσα μὲν δεῖ ἐργάζεσθαι ἐπάραντας τὸν ἀγκῶνα ἀπὸ τῶν πλευρέων εἰς τὸ πλάγιον, ταῦτα μὲν οὐ δύναται ἅπαντα ὁμοίως ἐργάζεσθαι. ὁκόσα δὲ δεῖ ἐργάζεσθαι παραφέροντας τὸν βραχίονα παρὰ τὰς πλευρὰς ἢ ἐς τοὐπίσω ἢ ἐς τοὔμπροσθεν. ταῦτα δὲ δύνανται ἐργάζεσθαι· καὶ γὰρ ἂν ἀρίδα ἑλκύσαιεν καὶ πρίονα καὶ πελεκίσαιεν ἂν καὶ σκάψαιεν, ἂν μὴ κάρτα ἄνω αἴροντες τὸν ἀγκῶνα καὶ τἄλλα ὅσα τῶν τοιούτων σχημάτων ἐργάζονται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀρίδα, ἕξις

397 In those in whom the shoulder has been dislocated when they are already grown men and has not been reduced, the epomis becomes less fleshy and the habit of body in that region thin—when, however, they have ceased from being in pain. Whatever tasks must be performed by raising the elbow away from the ribs toward the side, all these they cannot perform in like manner; but whatever tasks must be performed by carrying the upper arm along past the ribs, either backward or forward, these they can perform; for they could draw a drill (ἀρίς) and a saw, and could hew with an axe and dig, provided they do not raise the elbow very much upward, and the other things, as many as are done in such postures.

Greek (Kühn)

Ἡ ἐπωμὶς τοῖς οὕτω πεπονθόσι κἀν τῷ παραχρῆμα φαίνηται λεπτοτέρα καὶ ἀσαρκωτέρα τῆς κεφαλῆς τοῦ βραχίονος ἀπολειπούσης τὸν ὦμον, ἀλλὰ καὶ εἰς ὕστερον ἔτι μᾶλλον φαίνεται τοιαύτη, διότι γίνεται τοῦ χρόνου 398 περιόντος ἀτροφώτερα τὰ μόρια τοῦ δελτοειδοῦς μυὸς ἀσθενοῦς τεινομένου, διότι κάτω γίνεται βίαιος ἀκολουθῶν εἰς μασχάλην τῆς κεφαλῆς τοῦ βραχίονος. ὅτι δὲ διὰ τὸ μὴ κινεῖσθαι μηδὲ ἐνεργεῖν τὴν οἰκείαν ἐνέργειαν, ἣν εἶχεν ἀνατείνων τὸν βραχίονα, ψυχρότερος καὶ ἀσθενέστερος ἀποτενούμενος καὶ ἀτροφώτερος διὰ τοῦτο γίνεται· κίνησις μὲν γὰρ κρατύνει, ἀργίη δὲ τήκει. Ὅσοισι δ’ ἂν τὸ ἀκρώμιον ἀποσπασθῇ, τουτέοισι φαίνεται ἐξέχον τὸ ὀστέον τῶν ἀπεσπασμένων. ἔστι δὲ τοῦτο ὁ σύνδεσμος τῆς κληῖδος καὶ τῆς ὠμοπλάτης· ἑτεροίη γὰρ φύσις ἀνθρώπου ταύτη ἢ τῶν ἄλλων ζώων. οἱ οὖν ἰητροὶ μάλιστα ἐξαπατέονται ἐν τούτῳ τῷ τρώματι, ἅτε γὰρ ἀνασχόντος τοῦ ὀστέου τοῦ ἀποσπασθέντος ἡ ἐπωμὶς φαίνεται χαμαιζήλη καὶ κοίλη, ὥστε καὶ προμηθεῖσθαι τῶν ὤμων τῶν ἐκπεπτωκότων.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀποτενούμενος, ὅτι δὲ διὰ τὸ μὴ κινεῖσθαι, προμηθεῖσθαι

The epomis, in those so affected, even if at the very moment it appears thinner and more fleshless, since the head of the humerus has abandoned the shoulder, yet afterwards appears still more so, because, as time 398 goes on, the parts become more atrophied, the deltoid muscle being weakly stretched, because it is forcibly drawn downward, following the head of the humerus into the armpit. And it is also the case that, because it is not moved and does not perform its proper activity, which it had in raising up the upper arm, it becomes colder and weaker, being stretched out (ἀποτενούμενος), and on this account more atrophied; for motion strengthens, while idleness wastes. In those whose acromion has been torn away, the bone of the parts torn away appears to project. This is the connection of the clavicle and the scapula; for the nature of man is different in this respect from that of the other animals. Physicians, therefore, are most deceived in this injury; for since the bone that has been torn away stands up, the epomis appears low-lying and hollow, so that they even take precautions as for shoulders that have been dislocated.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κἀν τῷ παραχρῆμα φαίνηται, ἀσθενοῦς τεινομένου, βίαιος, ὅτι δὲ, ἀποτενούμενος, σύνδεσμος, προμηθεῖσθαι

In those so affected, the point of the shoulder appears thinner and less fleshy even immediately, since the head of the upper arm has left the shoulder; but later on it appears still more so, because, as time 398 goes on, the parts of the deltoid muscle become less nourished, the muscle being weakly stretched, because it is forcibly drawn downward, following the head of the upper arm into the armpit. And also because, through not moving and not exercising its proper activity, which it used to exercise in raising the upper arm, it becomes colder and weaker, being stretched out, and for this reason less nourished; for movement strengthens, but idleness wastes away. In those in whom the acromion has been torn away, the bone that has been torn away appears projecting. This is the junction of the collarbone and the shoulder blade; for the nature of man is different in this respect from that of the other animals. Physicians, then, are especially deceived in this injury; for since the bone that has been torn away stands up, the point of the shoulder appears low-lying and hollow, so that they even make provision as for shoulders that have been dislocated.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: δοκέοντας, ἀποπασθῇ, εἰ ἄλλως ἐθέλοις

I know, then, many physicians, not bad in other respects, who have already done much harm in trying to reduce such shoulders, supposing them to have been dislocated in this way, 399 and they do not stop before either giving up or being at a loss, believing that they themselves are reducing the shoulder. For these the treatment is the same as for the other such cases: cerate, compresses, linen bandages, and bandaging of that kind. One must, however, press down the projecting part, place the compresses there in greatest number and apply pressure at these points, and above all keep the upper arm attached to the sides and held in the upward direction; for in this way the torn-away part would most closely be brought near its place. These things, indeed, one must know well and predict as certain, if one wishes besides: that no harm, either small or great, comes to the shoulder from this injury, but that the place is uglier. For this bone would not be settled into its original seat in the same way as it is by nature, but it must necessarily be more or less bulging upward. For neither does any other bone return to the same position, whenever, being in connection with another bone and grown onto it, it is torn away (ἀποπασθῇ, for ἀποσπασθῇ) from its original nature.

Now I know many physicians, not otherwise incompetent, who have already done much harm in attempting to reduce such shoulders, supposing them to be 399 dislocated; and they do not leave off until they either despair or are at a loss, believing that they themselves have reduced the shoulder. For these patients the treatment is the same as for other such injuries: cerate, compresses, bandages, and bandaging of the same kind. One must, however, press down the projecting part, place the greatest number of compresses over it, and press upon these, and above all keep the upper arm attached to the ribs, held in the upward direction; for in this way the part that has been torn away would best be brought close again. These things, however, one must know well and foretell as certain, if you wish otherwise: that no harm, either small or great, comes to the shoulder from this injury, but the place is more unsightly. For this bone could not be settled in its original seat in the same way as it is by nature, but it must necessarily be more or less more prominent upwards. For no other bone either is restored to the same position, when, being in union with another bone and grown onto it, it is torn away from its original nature.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: Σύνζευξιν

And the acromion becomes painless 400 within a few days, if it is bandaged properly. He indicates that the acromion is a conjunction, and he says also that it is a bone and that it binds together both of the parts mentioned. It is clear, therefore, that the acromion is a cartilaginous bone lying upon the conjunction of the clavicle and the scapula, having membranes growing out from it, by which the extremities of the bones are bound together. When it has been torn away, the clavicle too is accustomed to follow along with it, and for this reason restoration comes about when the clavicle is forced downwards, so that certain circular bendings of it are produced. Here indeed this forcing-down succeeds better in the younger; for the further age advances, the drier our bodies become.

The acromion 400 becomes painless within a few days, if it is bandaged properly. He indicates that the acromion is a junction, and he also says that it is a bone and that it binds together both of the parts mentioned. It is clear, then, that the acromion is a cartilaginous bone lying upon the junction of the collarbone and the shoulder blade, having membranes growing out from it, by which the ends of the bones are bound together. When it is torn away, the collarbone too usually follows along with it, and for this reason correction is effected by forcing the collarbone downwards, so that certain rounded curvatures of it are produced. In this case, indeed, this forcing down succeeds better in younger people; for the further age advances, the drier our bodies become.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπανορθωσάμενος

Just as dry pieces of wood, then, are not suited to bendings, whereas moist and green ones readily undergo this, in the same way the bones of those who are still growing can also be bent when they are forced down, and much more so when they are loose-textured and cavernous, as 401 the clavicle is. And I myself had experience of this in my own case, perceiving the clavicle being bent; for it throbbed in the depth whenever it was violently drawn down by the forcefulness of the bandaging, and I endured this for up to forty days, during which the clavicle was brought so close to the head of the humerus that those who see it now disbelieve that it was ever torn away, while those who saw it when it was torn away, an interval of three fingers having come about between, marvel exceedingly at the matter. It happened, however, that the upper arm wasted completely during that time, so as to become altogether thin, and the forearm wasted away along with it. But having set these things right by a single remedy, that of the bandaging, the whole limb was nourished back up, not long after the bandages were loosed. In the case of an adolescent, and still more in the case of children, the bandaging would force down the bone more readily; for it befell me to suffer thus in the wrestling-school when I was in my thirty-fifth year, yet I was not able to cure in like manner any other of those already full-grown, because they did not bear the forcefulness of the bandaging.

I myself, at any rate, also felt the region to be altogether cold, so that I was compelled 402 throughout the whole day and night to pour warm oil over it, a hide having been spread beneath my whole body, for I lay naked upon it, since indeed it was the season of the burning heat under the Dog-star. ἀποσπασθέντος γὰρ τοῦ ἀκρωμίου κατὰ παλαίστραν ὁ παιδοτρίβης ἰδὼν ταπεινὴν τὴν ἐπωμίδα καὶ εἰς μασχάλην ὠλισθηκέναι τὸν βραχίονα κατέτεινέ τε καὶ μοχλεύειν ἐπεχείρει καὶ τεχνικῶς ἐνήργει 403 ταῦτα. μὴ προχωροῦντος δὲ τοῦ ἔργου καὶ μάτην τοῦ χρόνου τριβομένου νομίσας αὐτὸν ἐν τῇ μοχλείᾳ τι σφάλλεσθαι, τοὺς μὲν ἄλλους μετ’ ἐκείνου τείνειν τε τὸν βραχίονα καὶ ἀντιτείνειν ἄνω τὸν ὦμον ἐκέλευον, αὐτὸς ὑποβαλὼν τῆς ἀπαθοῦς χειρὸς τοὺς δακτύλους ὡς ἐσωτάτω τοῦ δοκοῦντος ὠλισθηκέναι τὸν βραχίονα, ἵνα μεταξὺ τῶν πλευρῶν καὶ τῆς κεφαλῆς αὐτοὺς παρενθεὶς ἀναμοχλεύσειεν αὐτὸ μικρὸν τοῦτο μηδὲν εὑρὼν ἐν τῇ μασχάλῃ παρὰ φύσιν ἐκέλευον ἐπισχεῖν τὴν τάσιν τοὺς ἅμα τῷ παιδοτρίβῃ τείνοντάς τε καὶ ἀντιτείνοντας, οὐ γὰρ ἐκπεπτωκέναι τὸ ἄρθρον. οἱ δ’ ὡς μετὰ τὴν ὀδύνην μαλθακιζομένου μου περιέτρεπόν τε παρέχειν αὐτὸν ἑαυτοῖς οὐκ ἐμαυτοῦ, οὐκ ἐπαύοντό τε τείνοντες, ὥς τις ἂν διέσπασε τὸν μῦν, ὡς ἤδη καὶ ἄλλοις τισὶ συνέπεσεν, εἰ μὴ κατὰ τύχην ἐπιφανὴς ἕτερος εὖ ἰδὼν, μηδέποτ’ ἂν διὰ μαλακίαν εἶρξα αὐτοὺς τῆς τάσεως, ἀπέλυσε μὲν ἐκείνους, ἐμοὶ δ’ ἐπύθετο τί ποτ’ ἂν εἵη τὸ γεγονὸς, ὅ τι τε κελεύοι με πράττειν.

For when the acromion had been torn away in the wrestling-school, the trainer, seeing that the epomis was low and that the humerus had slipped into the armpit, both extended it and set about levering it, and he carried out 403 these operations skilfully. But as the work was not succeeding and time was being wasted to no purpose, I supposed that he was going wrong in some respect in the levering, and I ordered the others to extend the arm together with him and to counter-extend the shoulder upwards, while I myself slipped the fingers of my unaffected hand as far inwards as possible beneath the place where the humerus seemed to have slipped, in order that, by inserting them between the ribs and the head, I might lever it up a little (ἀναμοχλεύσειεν); but finding nothing in the armpit that was contrary to nature, I ordered those who were extending and counter-extending along with the trainer to stop the extension, for the joint had not been dislocated. They, however, on the assumption that I was going soft after the pain, kept trying to turn me round (περιέτρεπον) into surrendering it to themselves and not to me, and did not stop extending, so that someone would have torn the muscle apart, as has in fact already happened to certain others, had not by chance another man, a distinguished one, who saw well that I would never have restrained them from the extension out of softness, dismissed those men and inquired of me what the thing that had happened might be and what I would bid him do (ὅ τι τε κελεύοι με πράττειν).

For when the acromion had been torn away in the palaestra, the trainer, seeing the point of the shoulder lowered and supposing the upper arm had slipped into the armpit, both applied extension and attempted to lever it, and he performed these things skilfully. 403 But since the work made no progress and the time was being spent in vain, I thought that he was going somewhat wrong in the levering, and I ordered the others, together with him, to extend the upper arm and to counter-extend the shoulder upward. I myself placed the fingers of my unaffected hand as far inward as possible toward the place where the upper arm seemed to have slipped, so that by inserting them between the ribs and the head I might lever it up a little; and finding nothing contrary to nature in the armpit, I ordered those who were extending and counter-extending together with the trainer to stop the extension, for the joint had not been dislocated. But they, as though I were going soft after the pain, kept turning me round (?) to hand myself over to them rather than to myself, and did not stop extending, so that one of them would have torn the muscle apart, as has already happened to certain others, had not by chance another man, a man of distinction, seeing clearly that I would never have kept them from the extension out of softness, released them and asked me what in the world had happened and what I bade him do.

So I told him that what had happened was that the acromion had been torn away, and that I needed oil and 404 linen bandage and wool; I myself went off to the bath and, having a great deal of oil poured over me, I lingered in the basin as long as was possible, being minded that the things required for the treatment should be fetched; so that, on account of this hardship too, my muscles needed continuous affusion. Most people, however, even when this has not occurred, do not tolerate the bandaging itself, but choose to have the trouble arising from the affection for the time thereafter rather than be put to pain for up to forty days; for I do not think that the bone of the clavicle can be exactly bent back (καμφθῆναι) in less than so many days. The rest of the passage is clear. A clavicle that has been fractured, if it is snapped clean off, is easier to treat; but if lengthwise, harder to treat.

I told him, then, that what had happened was a tearing away of the acromion, and that there was need of oil and 404 linen bandage and wool. Greek (Kühn)

τἀναντία δὲ τούτοις ἐστὶν ἢ ὡς ἄν τις οἴοιτο· τὴν μὲν γὰρ ἀτρεκέως ἀποκαυλισθεῖσαν προσαναγκάσειεν ἄν τις μᾶλλον ἐς τὴν φύσιν ἐλθεῖν καὶ εἰ πάνυ προμηθηθείη τὸ ἀνωτέρω, κατωτέρω ἂν ποιήσειε σχήμασί τε ἐπιτηδείοισι καὶ ἐπιδέσει 405 ἁρμοζούσῃ. εἰ δὲ μὴ τελέως ἱδρυνθείη, ἀλλ’ οὖν τὸ ὑπερέχον γε τοῦ ὀστέου οὐ κάρτα ὀξὺ γίνεται. ὡς δ’ ἂν παράμηκες τὸ ὀστέον κατεάγῃ, ἰκέλη ἡ ξυμφορὴ γίνεται τοῖσιν ὀστέοισι τοῖσιν ἀπεσπασμένοισι, περὶ ὧν πρόσθεν γέγραπται· οὕτε γὰρ ἱδρυνθῆναι αὐτὸ πρὸς ἑωυτὸ κάρτα ἐθέλει, ἥ τε ὑπερέχουσα ἀκρὶς τοῦ ὀστέου ὀξεῖα γίνεται κάρτα. τὸ μὲν οὖν σύμπαν εἰδέναι χρὴ ὅτι βλάβη οὐδεμίη τῷ ὤμῳ οὐδὲ τῷ ἄλλῳ σώματι γίνεται διὰ τὴν κάτηξιν τῆς κληῖδος, ἢν μὴ ἐπισφακελίσῃ. ὀλιγάκις δὲ τοῦτο γίνεται. Λέγει τὴν δι’ ὅλως ἀποκαυλισθεῖσαν, ὡς κατὰ μηδὲν προσέχεσθαι, τὸ δὲ ἀντικείμενον τούτῳ τὸ οὐκ ἀτρεκέως ἐστὶν, ὃ καθ’ ἕνα τρόπον γινόμενον ἐδήλωσεν εἰπών· ἢν δὲ παραμηκέως, δυσιητοτέρη μετ’ αὐτοῦ καὶ συνενδείξασθαι τὴν μὲν ἀκριβῶς ἀποκαυλιζομένην ἐγκαρσίαν πάσχειν τοῦτο, τὴν δ’ οὐκ ἀκριβῶς κατὰ μῆκος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: δι’ ὅλως, μετ’ αὐτοῦ καὶ συνενδείξασθαι, ἐπισφακελίσῃ

But these matters are the opposite of what one might suppose. For the one that has been snapped off clean one could rather force to come back into its natural position, and if one took very careful forethought for the upper part, one would bring it lower by suitable postures and by a bandaging 405 that fits. And even if it should not be completely settled, still at least the projecting part of the bone does not become very sharp. But when the bone is broken lengthwise, the mishap becomes like that of the bones which have been torn away, about which it has been written above; for it neither is very willing to be settled in relation to itself, and the projecting point of the bone becomes very sharp. On the whole, then, one must know that no harm comes to the shoulder or to the rest of the body on account of the fracture of the collarbone, unless it should become affected by sphakelos. And this happens rarely. He means the one snapped off completely (δι’ ὅλως), so that it adheres at no point; and the opposite of this is the one not snapped off clean, which he showed as occurring in one manner when he said, "but if lengthwise, it is harder to treat"—and along with this (μετ’ αὐτοῦ καὶ συνενδείξασθαι) he also jointly indicated that the one snapped off exactly suffers this transversely, while the one not snapped off exactly suffers it lengthwise.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: συνενδείξασθαι, ἐπισφακελίσῃ, ἀτρεκέως ἀποκαυλισθεῖσαν, ἀκρίς

The reverse of these is the case from what one might suppose. For the bone snapped off squarely one could more readily constrain to return to its natural position, and if one took great forethought, one could bring the higher part lower by suitable postures and by fitting 405 bandaging. And even if it should not be completely settled in place, at any rate the projecting part of the bone does not become very sharp. But when the bone is broken lengthwise, the mishap becomes like that of the bones that have been torn off, about which it has been written before. For it is not very ready to settle in place against itself, and the projecting point of the bone becomes very sharp. On the whole, then, one must know that no harm comes to the shoulder or to the rest of the body through the fracture of the clavicle, unless sphacelus supervenes; and this happens rarely. By 'squarely' he means the bone snapped off clean through, so that it holds together at no point. The opposite of this is 'not squarely', which he indicated as occurring in one manner when he said, 'but if lengthwise, harder to cure'. Together with this he also indicated that the bone snapped off exactly undergoes this transversely, while the one not snapped off exactly undergoes it lengthwise.

ὅταν μὲν οὖν ὁλοκλήρως ἀποκαυλισθῇ, παραλλάττειν εἴωθε τοὐπίπαν ἀλλήλων τὰ 406 μόρια, καὶ τὸ μὲν ἄνω χωρεῖν, τὸ δὲ κάτω, τὸ μὲν ἔσω, τὸ δὲ ἕξω. καὶ κατὰ τοῦτο φαίνεται μέγα τε τὸ κάταγμα καὶ δυσίατον εἶναι, κατὰ δὲ τὸν ἕτερον τρόπον, ἡνίκα παραμήκης ἐστὶν ἡ διάθεσις τοῦ κατάγματος, ἡ μὲν φαντασία μικροτέρα, χαλεπωτέρα δὲ ἡ ἴασις. οὐ γὰρ ἐνδέχεται τὴν ἐν κύκλῳ περιβολὴν ὥσπερ τὰ κῶλα μόνην δυναμένην προστεῖλαι καὶ παραγαγεῖν τὸ παρὰ μῆκος ἐσχισμένον τῆς κλειδός. ἐὰν οὖν, ὦν εἴρηκα νῦν εἴης μεμνημένος, οὐδὲν ἐνεγκεῖν φαίνεται τῶν ἐφεξῆς ὑπ’ αὐτοῦ λεγομένων ἀσαφές. Αἶσχός γε μὴν προσγίνεται περὶ τὴν κάτηξιν τῆς κληῖδος καὶ τούτοισι τοῖσι τὸ πρῶτον αἴσχιστον, ἔπειτα μὴν ἐπὶ ἧσσον γίνεται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὥσπερ τὰ κῶλα μόνην δυναμένην, τούτοισι τοῖσι

When, then, it has been broken clean off in its entirety, the parts 406 are generally accustomed to shift past one another, and the one to move upward, the other downward, the one inward, the other outward. And in this respect the fracture appears to be both great and hard to cure; whereas in the other manner, when the disposition of the fracture is lengthwise, the appearance is smaller, but the cure is more difficult. For it is not possible for the encircling wrapping, which alone is able to do so as in the case of the limbs, to compress and bring back into place the part of the clavicle that has been split along its length. If, then, you keep in mind what I have now said, nothing of what is said by him in what follows will appear to present anything unclear. "Ugliness, however, is added in the case of fracture of the clavicle, and for these persons (τούτοισι τοῖσι) at first it is most ugly, but thereafter it becomes less."

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐνδέχεται, περιβολὴν, παραγαγεῖν, ὦν, ἐνεγκεῖν, τούτοισι τοῖσι, ἔπειτα μὴν ἐπὶ ἧσσον

When, then, it is broken off completely, the parts are as a rule wont to be displaced from one another, the one moving upward and the other downward, the one inward and the other outward. In this case the fracture appears to be both large and hard to cure; but in the other mode, when the disposition of the fracture is lengthwise, the appearance is smaller, yet the cure more difficult. For it is not possible for the circular wrapping, which alone is able, as in the case of the limbs, to press down and draw into place, [to do so for] the part of the clavicle that is split lengthwise. If, then, you remember what I have just said, nothing of what he says next will appear to carry any obscurity. "Disfigurement, however, does accrue in connection with the fracture of the clavicle, and in these cases it is at first most unsightly, but afterwards it becomes less so."

Greek (Kühn)

Βέλτιον ἦν εἰπεῖν ἐπὶ ἧσσον γίνεται· κατ’ ἀρχὰς μὲν γὰρ τῷ ξένῳ τοῦ πράγματος αἰσχρὸν αὐτοῖς εἶναι δοκεῖ τὸ 407 γεγονός. ἐν δὲ τῷ χρόνῳ συνειθιζομένοις ὥσπερ ἐπὶ τοῖς ἄλλοις ἅπασιν, οὕτω κἀπὶ τῷδε καταφρονεῖται τὸ αἶσχος. Συμφύεται δὲ ταχέως κληῒς καὶ τἄλλα πάντα ὅσα χαῦνα ὀστέα. ταχεῖαν γὰρ τὴν ἐπιπώρωσιν ποιέεται τὰ τοιαῦτα. ὅταν μὲν οὖν νεωστὶ καταγῇ, οἱ τετρωμένοι σπουδάζουσιν οἰόμενοι μεῖζον τὸ κακὸν εἶναι ἢ ὅσον ἐστὶν οἵ τε ἰητροὶ προθυμέονται δῆθεν ὀρθῶς ἰῆσθαι. προϊόντος δὲ τοῦ χρόνου οἱ τετρωμένοι, ἅτε οὐκ ὀδυνώμενοι οὔτε κωλυόμενοι οὔτε ὁδοιπορίης οὔτε ἐδωδῆς καταμελέουσιν· οἵ τε αὖ ἰητροὶ ἅτε οὐ δυνάμενοι κατὰ τὰ χωρία ἀποδεικνύναι ἀποδιδράσκουσι καὶ οὐκ ἄχθονται τῇ ἀμελείῃ τῶν τετρωμένων, ἐν τούτῳ δὲ ἡ ἐπιπώρωσις συνταχύνεται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κατὰ τὰ χωρία ἀποδεικνύναι, δῆθεν

It would have been better to say "becomes less"; for at the beginning, because of the strangeness of the thing, what has happened seems to them to be 407 ugly. But in time, as they become accustomed to it, just as with everything else, so also in this case the ugliness comes to be disregarded. The collar-bone unites quickly, and so do all the other bones that are porous. For such bones produce a rapid formation of callus. When, then, it has been recently broken, the injured take the matter seriously, thinking the evil to be greater than it actually is, and the physicians, for their part, are eager, as it were, to treat it correctly. But as time goes on, the injured, inasmuch as they are neither in pain nor hindered either in walking or in eating, grow careless; and the physicians in turn, inasmuch as they are unable to make a showing in the affected parts, slip away and are not troubled by the carelessness of the injured, and in the meantime the formation of callus is hastened.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: Βέλτιον ἦν εἰπεῖν ἐπὶ ἧσσον γίνεται (the correction repeats the wording of the lemma as printed, so the lemma or the comment may be corrupt), κατὰ τὰ χωρία ἀποδεικνύναι (perhaps to be read καλὰ τὰ χωρία, 'to make the parts look well'), δῆθεν

It would have been better to say 'becomes less so'; for at first, because of the strangeness of the thing, what has 407 happened seems unsightly to them. But in time, as they grow accustomed to it, just as with all other things, so with this too the unsightliness comes to be disregarded. The clavicle unites quickly, as do all other bones that are porous; for such bones form callus quickly. Now when it has been freshly broken, the injured are anxious, thinking the trouble greater than it is, and the physicians are eager, ostensibly, to treat it correctly. But as time goes on, the injured, since they feel no pain and are hindered neither in walking nor in eating, become neglectful; and the physicians in their turn, since they are unable to display the parts in their proper position, slip away and are not displeased at the neglect of the injured; and meanwhile the formation of callus is hastened.

The manner of bandaging, then, is established as similar to that in most cases: to treat with cerate and compresses and soft linen bandages; and these things must be treated in addition, and these things must be understood in addition, and most of all in this handling, 408 that one must place the greatest number of compresses over the projecting part, and one must press with the greatest number of bandages, and most of all at this point. There are indeed some who have already devised the refinement of binding on in addition a heavy piece of lead, so as to force down the projecting part. They bandage them, then, using compresses and linen bandages, just as the others do; but having girded the man with a certain band at the place where he is of himself most firmly girdable, when they have laid the compresses upon the projecting parts of the fracture, heaping them up over the parts that stand out, they fastened the beginning of the linen bandage to the girdle from the front, and so they bandage, stretching it along the line of the collar-bone and carrying it backwards, and then, having passed it round the 409 girdle, they bring it forward and again backward. Some, however, do not pass the linen bandage round the girdle, but, carrying the bandage in a circle round the perineum and alongside the seat itself and along the spine, in this way they compress the fracture. These things, at any rate, seem to one without experience, on hearing them, to be close to what is in accordance with nature, but to one who uses them they are useless; for they are not stable for any length of time, not even if one were to lie down—although in that way it would come nearest—but nevertheless if, while lying down, he either bent the leg or were himself bent, all the bandages would be displaced; and besides, the bandaging is troublesome (ἀσηρή). He himself stated the cause of such fractures uniting quickly; the cause, however, of the formation of callus being rapid he did not go on to state, as being one that could be understood from the very nature of the bones about which the discourse is. For the spongy bones have a greater share of moisture in them.

They bandage them, then, using compresses and linen bandages, just as the others do. But first they gird the man with a band, at the place where he is most easily girded. When they have laid the compresses on the projecting parts of the fracture, building them up over the prominences, they tie the beginning of the linen bandage to the girdle from the front. He himself stated the cause of such parts uniting quickly; but the cause of the formation of callus being rapid he did not go on to state, as being something that can be understood from the very nature of the bones under discussion. And it has been said by us in what precedes that, when this [moisture] is poured out and then congealed by the faculty residing in the affected bone, the 410 callus grows upon the lips of the fractured bone. All that follows is clear to those who apply their mind. "For the seat is intercepted, and the bandages come to be gathered all together in that narrow place; and again, those cast around the girdle have not been girt so strongly as not to compel the girdle to ride upward, and thus it would be necessary that all the bandages slacken. One would seem to come nearest, though not doing anything great, if one were to cast some of the bandages around the girdle, but with most of the bandages apply the ancient bandaging; for thus the bandages would be most stable and would assist one another."

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: λέγονται δὶς ἐν, ψιλοῦται

Most of the things, then, have been stated that befall 411 those whose clavicle is fractured; but one must also understand this in addition, that the clavicle is for the most part fractured in such a way that the bone growing from the chest projects toward the upper part, while the bone growing from the acromion lies in the lower part. The causes of these things are the following: that the chest would not move much either downward or upward. The word στενυγροχωρίῳ is not a compound noun formed from ὑγρόν (moist) and στενόν (narrow) and χώρα (place), as some think, but comes from στενυγρόν, which itself signifies nothing more than στενόν (narrow), στενυγροχωρία having come into being by a certain derivation and indicating nothing more than narrowness. This is also quite clear from what Simonides said, the words running roughly thus: 'Not yet did any man thus in the shadowy mountains, λέγονται δὶς ἐν [corrupt; perhaps 'fear a lion'], nor, alone, a leopard, meeting it in a narrow (στενυγρῷ) path.' And it evidently takes the smooth breathing in the second syllable.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: στενυγροχωρίῳ, λέγονται δὶς ἐν, οὔπω τις οὕτω δασκίοις ἐν οὔρεσιν ἀνὴρ, κατὰ παραγωγήν τινα γεγενημένης, ψιλοῦται

Now, most of the things that befall 411 those who break the collarbone have been stated; but one must also understand this further point, that the collarbone for the most part breaks in such a way that the bone growing from the chest projects toward the upper part, while the bone growing from the acromion lies in the lower part. The causes of these things are these: that the chest could move neither much lower nor much higher. The word stenygrochōriē ('in a narrow space') is not a compound formed from hygron ('moist'), stenon ('narrow') and chōra ('place'), as some suppose. Rather, stenygrochōria has come about by a kind of derivation from stenygron, which itself signifies nothing more than stenon ('narrow'), so that stenygrochōria indicates nothing more than narrowness. This is quite clear also from what Simonides said, which runs roughly thus: 'never yet has any man thus in the shadowy mountains [λέγονται δὶς ἐν: corrupt] nor a leopard, alone, meeting it on a narrow (stenygros) path.' And the word evidently has the smooth breathing at the second syllable.

412 Μικρὸς γὰρ ὁ κιγκλισμὸς τοῦ ἄρθρου τοῦ ἐν τῷ στήθει. Τὴν βραχεῖαν κίνησιν ὠνόμασε κιγκλισμόν. εἰ δ’ ἀπὸ τοῦ ζώου τοῦ κίγκλου γέγονε τοὔνομα, διότι κινεῖ συνεχῶς ἐκεῖνο τὰ πηγάνεια πτέρνα ἢ ἀπό τινος ἄλλου τῆς προκειμένης πραγματείας, οὐκ ἔστιν οἰκεῖον, ἀλλὰ μᾶλλον δεῖται αἰτίαν εἰπεῖν, δι’ ἣν ἡ πρὸς τὸ στέρνον τῆς κλειδὸς διάρθρωσις οὐ τῶν μικροτάτων οὖσα, καθάπερ ἕτεραί τινες, ὅμως οὐκ ἔχει κίνησιν ἀξιόλογον. αὐτὸς οὖν ὁ Ἱπποκράτης ἥντινα λέγει τούτου τὴν αἰτίαν εἶναι διὰ τῆς λέξεως ἀκούσωμεν. στῆθος δὲ ἔοικεν οὐ τὸ σύμπαν λέγειν χωρίον, ὅσον ἐν τοῖς πρόσω τοῦ θώρακός ἐστιν, ἀλλὰ τὸ μέσον ὀστοῦν, ἐν ᾧ διαρθροῦνται πλευραὶ, ὃ καλοῦσιν ἰδίως οἱ μετ’ αὐτὸν ἰατροὶ στέρνον, ὅπερ ἀληθῶς εἶπεν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πηγάνεια, πτέρνα

412 For the kinklismos of the joint at the breast is small. He called the slight movement "kinklismos." But whether the name arose from the animal, the kinklos, because that bird continually moves its tail-feathers (πηγάνεια πτέρνα), or from something else, is not pertinent to the present treatise; rather, what is needed is to state the cause on account of which the articulation of the clavicle with the sternum, although it is not among the smallest, as certain others are, nevertheless does not have a movement worth mentioning. Let us therefore hear from the text itself what Hippocrates himself says the cause of this is. By "breast" he seems to mean not the whole region which lies in the front parts of the thorax, but the middle bone in which the ribs are articulated, which the physicians after him specifically call the sternum; and what he said is true.

412 For the oscillation of the joint at the chest is small. He has called a slight movement *kinklismos* ('wagging'). Whether the name derives from the animal *kinklos* (the wagtail), because that bird continually moves its rump(?) feathers, or from something else, is not germane to the present treatise. What is needed instead is to state the cause on account of which the articulation of the collarbone with the breastbone, although it is not among the smallest, as certain others are, nevertheless has no appreciable movement. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πλοώδης

413 For the chest is continuous with itself and with the spine. That the sternum is continuous with itself is plainly apparent; that it is continuous with the spine will be clearly understood if you ever see, on a skeletonised animal, the ribs plainly fixed behind upon the spine and in front against the sternum. Nearest to the joint of the shoulder the clavicle is mobile; for it is compelled to be frequently moved because of its conjunction with the acromion. Besides, when it is injured, the part attached to the chest escapes toward the upper side and is not at all willing to be forced toward the lower side. For it is naturally light, and the roomy space for it is greater above than below. But the shoulder and the parts attached to these are easily detached from the ribs and the chest, and for this reason it can be drawn both much higher and lower.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πλοώδης, σκελετῷ ζώῳ, τρωθῇ, εὐαπόλυτα

413 For the chest is continuous both with itself and with the spine. That the breastbone is continuous with itself is plainly evident; that it is continuous with the spine will be clearly understood if you ever see, in a skeleton of an animal, the ribs plainly resting behind upon the spine and in front against the breastbone. Nearest to the joint of the shoulder the collarbone is loose (?); for it is compelled to be frequently moved on account of its junction with the acromion. Besides, when it is injured, the part attached at the chest escapes upward, and is not very willing to be forced into the lower position. For it is naturally light, and the open space for it is greater above than below. But the shoulder and the parts attached to these are easily detached from the ribs and the chest, and for this reason it can be drawn both much higher and lower.

Greek (Kühn)

ὅταν 414 γοῦν καταγῇ κληῒς, τὸ πρὸς τῷ ὤμῳ ὀστέον ἐς τὸ κατωτέρω ἐπιῤῥέπει· ἐς τοῦτο γὰρ ἐπιτροχώτερον αὐτὸ ἅμα τῷ ὤμῳ καὶ τῷ βραχίονι κάτω ῥεῦσαι μᾶλλον ἢ ἐς τὸ ἄνω. ὁπότε οὖν ταῦτά ἐστιν, ἀξυνετέουσιν ὅσοι τὸ ὑπερέχον τοῦ ὀστέου ἐς τὸ κάτω καταναγκάσαι οἴονται εἶναι, ἀλλὰ δῆλον ὅτι τὸ κάτω πρὸς τῷ ἄνω προσακτέον ἐστί. τοῦτο γὰρ ἔχει κίνησιν, τοῦτο γάρ ἐστι καὶ τὸ ἀποστὰν ἀπὸ τῆς φύσιος. δῆλον οὖν ὅτι ἄλλως μὲν οὐδαμῶς ἐστιν ἀναγκάσαι τοῦτο· αἵ τε γὰρ ἐπιδέσιες οὐδέν τι μᾶλλον προσαναγκάζουσιν ἢ ἀπαναγκάζουσιν. εἰ δέ τις τὸν βραχίονα πρὸς τῇσι πλευρῇσιν ὡς μάλιστα ἐόντα ἀναγκάζοι ἄνω, ὡς ὅτι ὀξύτατος ὁ ὦμος φαίνεται εἶναι, δῆλον ὅτι οὕτως ἂν ἁρμοσθείη πρὸς τὸ ὀστέον τὸ ἀπὸ τοῦ στήθεος πεφυκὸς ὅθεν ἀπεσπάσθη.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

When, 414 at any rate, a clavicle is broken, the bone next to the shoulder inclines toward the lower side; for it is more apt to slip in this direction, downward, together with the shoulder and the upper arm, rather than upward. Since, then, these things are so, those who think that the projecting part of the bone must be forced downward lack understanding; rather, it is clear that the lower part is to be brought up to the upper. For this is what has movement, and this is also what has departed from its natural position. It is clear, then, that there is no other way at all to force this; for the bandagings press it toward no more than they press it away. But if someone, with the upper arm kept as close as possible to the ribs, should force it upward, so that the shoulder appears to be as pointed as possible, it is clear that in this way it would be fitted to the bone that grows from the chest, from which it was torn away.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπιτροχώτερον, ἐπιῤῥέπει

When, 414 at any rate, the collarbone is broken, the bone toward the shoulder inclines downward; for it is more readily disposed to slide downward together with the shoulder and the upper arm than upward. Since, then, these things are so, those who think that the projecting part of the bone is to be forced downward are without understanding; rather, it is clear that the lower part must be brought up to the upper. For this is the part that has movement; this is also the part that has departed from its natural position. It is clear, then, that there is no other way at all of forcing this into place; for bandagings do not press it toward the other part any more than they press it away. But if one were to force the upper arm upward while it is as close as possible to the ribs, so that the shoulder appears as sharp as possible, it is clear that in this way it would be fitted to the bone that grows from the chest, from which it was torn away.

Greek (Kühn)

Τροπικοῖς ὀνόμασι καὶ γλωσσηματικοῖς εἴωθεν ὁ Ἱπποκράτης ἐννοιῶν χρῆσθαι, καίτοι πολιτικὴν ἑρμηνεύων ἑρμηνείαν, ὅμοιόν τι τούτῳ πεπονθὼς ὁ Ξενοφῶν· καὶ 415 γὰρ ἐκεῖνος εἴπερ τις καὶ ἄλλος ἑρμηνεύων πολιτικῶς, ὅμως παρεμβάλλει πολλάκις ὀνόματα γλωσσηματικὰ καὶ τροπικά. σπανιώτερον μὲν οὖν παρ’ ἐκείνῳ τὸ τοιοῦτον, συνεχέστερον δὲ παρὰ τῷ Ἱπποκράτει, καθάπερ ἐπὶ τῇ προγεγραμμένῃ ῥήσει τὸν κιγκλισμὸν εἶπεν, ἐνταῦθά τε τὸ πλοώδης ἐστίν. ἐνδείκνυται γὰρ διὰ τῆς φωνῆς τὸ οἷον ἀστήρικτος, ᾧ καὶ τὸ ζῶον φαινόμενον μαρτυρεῖ, καθ’ ὅ φησιν ἠνάγκαστο, καθ’ ὃ πυκινοκίνητος αὐτὸς ἐδήλωσεν εἰπὼν, διὰ τὴν τῆς ἀκρωμίης σύζευξιν. αὕτη δὲ πάλιν ἡ ἀκρωμίη διὰ τὸ κατ’ ὦμον ἄρθρον ἐστὶ πυκινοκίνητος, ἀτενὴς, ἅτε ὑπάρχων αὐτὸς καὶ μεγίστας ἔχων κινήσεις. τὸ δὲ κατ’ ἀρχὴν εἰρημένον τῆς ῥήσεως, τὸ ἄγχιστα μὲν τὴν ἀμφιβολίαν ἣν ἔχει πρότερον ἐπὶ τοῦ χωρίου προσῆκεν ἀκούειν ἢ τοῦ πράγματος αὐτοῦ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐννοιῶν, ἠνάγκαστο, ἀτενὴς, ἅτε ὑπάρχων αὐτὸς

Hippocrates is accustomed to use figurative (tropical) and glossematic words for his notions, even though he employs the ordinary (civil) mode of expression; Xenophon has undergone something similar to this. For 415 he too, though if anyone ever did he expresses himself in the ordinary manner, nevertheless frequently inserts glossematic and figurative words. In his case, then, such a thing is rarer, but in Hippocrates it is more continual, just as in the passage written above he said kinklismos ("rocking"), and here there is the word ploōdēs ("floating"). For he indicates by this expression something like "unsupported," to which also what is observed in the living being bears witness, in respect of which he says "it was compelled," and in respect of which it is pukinokinētos ("much moved"), as he himself made clear when he said, "because of its conjunction with the acromion." And this acromion in turn is much moved because of the joint at the shoulder, being strained (atenēs), inasmuch as that joint is itself such and has the greatest movements. As for what is said at the beginning of the passage, the word anchista ("nearest"): the ambiguity which it has, it was fitting to understand it with reference to the place rather than to the thing itself.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ζῶον φαινόμενον, ἠνάγκαστο, καθ’ ὃ πυκινοκίνητος αὐτὸς ἐδήλωσεν, ἀτενὴς, ἅτε ὑπάρχων αὐτὸς, τὸ ἄγχιστα μὲν τὴν ἀμφιβολίαν ἣν ἔχει, κιγκλισμὸν, πλοώδης

Hippocrates is accustomed to use figurative and glossematic words for his ideas, even though he writes in a civic style of expression. Xenophon is in a similar case: 415 he too, if anyone does, expresses himself in the civic manner, yet he often inserts glossematic and figurative words. This is rarer with Xenophon and more frequent with Hippocrates. So in the passage written earlier he used the word 'kinklismos' (wagtail-like flicking), and here there is 'plōōdēs' ('floating'). By this word he indicates something like 'unsupported'. The appearance of the animal [ζῶον φαινόμενον, text doubtful] also bears witness to this. With regard to this he says 'it had been forced', and with regard to this he himself made the point clear by saying 'frequently moved' [πυκινοκίνητος], on account of its junction with the acromion. This acromion in turn is frequently moved, and taut [ἀτενής, sense doubtful], on account of the shoulder joint, since the joint itself [ἅτε ὑπάρχων αὐτός, construction doubtful] also has the greatest movements. As for what is said at the beginning of the passage, 'ἄγχιστα μέν' ('nearest'), given the ambiguity it carries, it ought rather to be understood of the place than of the thing itself.

δῆλον μὲν γὰρ ἐγγυτάτω, ἀλλ’ ἤτοι πλησίον τοῦ ἄρθρου πυκινοκίνητον ἐνδείξεται τὴν κλεῖν εἰρημένον, ὅπερ ἐμοὶ δοκεῖ μᾶλλον ἄν τις ἀκούῃ ὀρθῶς, ἐνίοις ἔδοξε συνάπτειν τὸ ἄγχιστα, δεήσει τὸ πυκινοκίνητον εἶναι 416 τὸ λεγόμενον, τὸ οὕτω ἐγγυτάτω τὸ πυκινοκινήτου ἡ κλείς ἐστι πλησίον τοῦ κατ’ ὦμον ἄρθρου. Εἰ οὖν τις τῇ μὲν ἐπιδέσει χρῷτο τῇ νομίμῃ τοῦ ταχέως συναλθεσθῆναι εἵνεκα, ἡγήσαιτο δὲ τἄλλα πάντα μάτην εἶναι παρὰ τὸ σχῆμα τὸ εἰρημένον, ὀρθῶς τε ἂν ξυνίῃ, ἰητρεύοι τ’ ἂν τάχιστα καὶ κάλλιστα· κατακέεσθαι μέντοι τὸν ἄνθρωπον μέγα τὸ διάφορόν ἐστι καὶ ἡμέραι ἱκαναὶ τεσσαρεσκαίδεκα, εἰ ἀτρεμέοι, εἴκοσι δὲ πάμπολλαι. Νομίμην ἐπίδεσιν λέγει τὴν ὑπὸ πάντων εἰθισμένην καὶ ὥσπερ ἄγραφον ἤδη νόμον οὖσαν· οὕτως γὰρ κἀν τῷ βίῳ τινὰ τῶν ἐθῶν, ὅσα πρὸς ἁπάντων ἐπιτηδεύηται, τοῖς νόμοις ἐστὶ παραπλήσια. τὴν μὲν οὖν καταγματικὴν ἐπίδεσιν οὐ πάντῃ ἐποίουν τοῦ προσήκοντος, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο πλείονα περὶ αὐτῆς ἔγραψεν ἐν πρώτῳ βιβλίῳ περὶ τῶν ἀγμῶν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἤτοι πλησίον τοῦ ἄρθρου πυκινοκίνητον ἐνδείξεται τὴν κλεῖν εἰρημένον, τὸ οὕτω ἐγγυτάτω τὸ πυκινοκινήτου, οὐ πάντῃ ἐποίουν τοῦ προσήκοντος

For it is clear that it means "nearest"; but either, said of the place, it will indicate that the clavicle is much-moved near the joint — which seems to me rather how one would understand it correctly — or, as some have thought one should join "nearest" (to the thing itself), what is said will have to be "much-moved", in the sense that the clavicle is thus nearest to what is much-moved, 416 being near the joint at the shoulder. If, then, someone should use the customary bandaging for the sake of its being healed quickly, but considered everything else to be in vain apart from the position described, he would both understand rightly and would treat most quickly and most well; yet for the man to lie down makes a great difference, and fourteen days are sufficient, if he keeps still, and twenty are very many. He calls "customary" the bandaging that is habitual among all and is, as it were, already an unwritten law; for so too in life some of the customs, all those that are practised by everyone, are similar to laws. Now the fracture-bandaging they did not perform in every respect as was fitting, and for this reason he wrote more about it in the first book, On Fractures.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: δῆλον μὲν γὰρ ἐγγυτάτω, ἤτοι (without a corresponding ἤ), ὅπερ ἐμοὶ δοκεῖ μᾶλλον ἄν τις ἀκούῃ ὀρθῶς, ἐνίοις ἔδοξε συνάπτειν τὸ ἄγχιστα, τὸ οὕτω ἐγγυτάτω τὸ πυκινοκινήτου, συναλθεσθῆναι

For it is clear that it means 'nearest'; but either, if said with reference to the place, it will indicate that the collarbone is very mobile (πυκινοκίνητον) near the joint, which seems to me the more correct way to take it, if one understands it rightly; or, as some have thought, if one is to join 'nearest' (ἄγχιστα) with what follows, then 'very mobile' will have to be 416 what is meant, namely: in this way 'nearest' to the very mobile [joint] is the collarbone, near the shoulder joint. 'If, then, someone were to use the customary bandaging for the sake of a quick knitting together, but were to consider everything else, apart from the posture that has been described, to be useless, he would both understand rightly and treat most quickly and most finely; it makes a great difference, however, that the patient lie down; and fourteen days are sufficient, if he keeps still, and twenty are more than enough.' By 'customary bandaging' he means that which is habitually used by everyone and has by now become, as it were, an unwritten law; for so too in life certain customs, those practised by all, are closely akin to laws. Now the bandaging for fractures they did not perform altogether as it ought to be done, and for this reason he wrote more about it in the first book On Fractures.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: νομὴν, ὁμολογέει δὲ ὁμοῦ μὲν

But here no bandaging of any affection is of this kind 417. For it has been shown by me also through the commentaries on that book, and it has been recalled even now, that all the affections which fall under the manner of the fracture-bandaging were written about by him in that book, and all those in turn which fall under the remaining kind in this one. For the fracture-bandaging comes about when we wrap the linen bandages in a circle around the fractured part; the second form of bandaging, when we make the distribution onto other parts, and for the most part onto those opposite to the affected part, just as he also said in In the Surgery: "and the wrap around the other armpit agrees at the same time." If, however, in someone the clavicle should be broken in the opposite way—which does not happen very often—so that the bone from the chest has slipped under, while the bone from the acromion projects and rides upon the other, these cases at least would need no great treatment.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀνεμνήσθη, νομὴν, ὁμολογέει, ἐνταυθοῖ δὲ οὐδεμία παθήματος ἐπίδεσις οὐδενὸς τοιαύτη

Here, however, the bandaging of no affection is of this kind. 417 For I have shown this both in the commentaries on that book, and it has now been recalled as well: all the affections that fall under the method of fracture bandaging were written by him in that book, and all those, in turn, that fall under the remaining kind in this one. For fracture bandaging is done when we wrap the linen strips in a circle around the fractured part; the second kind of bandaging, when we carry the distribution onto other parts, and for the most part onto those opposite to the affected one, as he said also in the Surgery: 'and at the same time the wrapping around the other armpit is consistent with this.' If, however, in someone the collarbone should be fractured the opposite way, which does not very often happen, so that the bone from the chest has sunk beneath while the bone from the acromion projects and rides upon the other, these would require no great treatment.

For the shoulder itself, when it is let down, and 418 the upper arm would settle the bones against one another, and some slight bandaging would suffice, and the formation of callus would be a matter of a few days. Earlier he said that for the most part the part of the clavicle on the side of the sternum, whenever it is broken clean across through the whole of itself, shifts toward the upper region, while the part on the side of the acromion becomes lower. But now he writes about the rarer mode, according to which the part on the side of the acromion becomes higher and the part toward the sternum lower, which he also says is easier to cure. When it (the shoulder) is let down, the part of the clavicle too, as much of it as is continuous with the acromion, settles down together with it; and thus, having come level with the other part, it obtains both the bandaging and the treatment.

For the shoulder itself, when let go, together with 418 the upper arm, would settle the bones against one another; a simple bandaging would suffice, and the formation of callus would take only a few days. Earlier he said that, for the most part, when the collarbone is snapped right through its whole thickness, the part of the collarbone that comes from the sternum shifts to the upper region, while the part that comes from the acromion becomes lower. Now, however, he writes about the rarer mode, in which the part from the acromion becomes higher and the part next to the sternum lower; this he also says is easier to treat. When it (the shoulder) is let down, the part of the collarbone continuous with the acromion also settles down along with it. And thus, having come level with the other part, it receives the bandaging and the treatment.

But whichever of the upper parts, slipping aside, have come to the side or lower down, it would contribute to the setting-right if the man lay on his back, and something a little raised were placed under him at the space between the scapulae, so that the chest be as arched back as possible, and the upper arm, if one should draw it up 420 stretched out along the ribs. And the physician, with one hand placing the thenar of his hand upon the head of the humerus, should push it away, and with the other should set the broken bones in good order; in this way he would best bring them into their natural state. But, as has already been said, the upper bone is very much inclined to slip underneath downward. For most, then, once they have been bandaged, the posture helps: keeping the elbow close against the ribs, and so forcing the shoulder upward. So when the part of the clavicle continuous with the sternum slips aside either to the side or downward, then, he says, it jointly contributes to the setting-right if someone, laying the man on his back, places under him between the scapulae along the spine itself—a pillow, evidently, or a neck-cushion, or something of the kind—so that the whole thorax becomes bent back at this point. In those cases where the upper [fragments], slipping, have passed to the side or lower down, it would contribute to the correction if the person were to lie supine, and something a little higher were placed beneath him in the region between the shoulder blades, so that the chest is as far as possible bent over [περιρρηδές]; and [it would contribute] if someone were to draw the upper arm upward, stretched out alongside the ribs. 421 So, when the man has been positioned in this manner, he requires the physician with the one hand to draw up the head of the humerus, which is stretched out alongside the ribs, and to push it outward. For in this way the fractured parts of the clavicle would be most separated, the bent-back posture also contributing not a little to this. And with the other hand to mould and bring together toward one another the separated parts of the arm (τῆς χειρὸς), while drawing up the humerus stretched out alongside the ribs; but in the following passage he says that it is sometimes better: To bring the elbow across toward the chest, and to keep the extremity of the hand beside the acromion of the healthy shoulder. K18a.422-423K. XVIIIA 422–423, ch. 73 difficulty high (3.81/100; thin 2, absent 2) (a) pass (b) fail 3/7 (c) pass 10/12 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

422 Τὸ δὲ τοιοῦτον σχῆμα πρὸς τὴν ἐκτὸς χώραν ἀπάγει δηλονότι τὴν κεφαλὴν τοῦ βραχίονος, ὡς συναπάγεται καὶ τὸ τῆς κλειδὸς μέρος τὸ συνημμένον τῷ ἀκρωμίῳ, ἀλλ’ οὐκ εἶναι χρείαν φησὶ τοιούτου σχήματος ὡς τὸ πολύ. γίγνεσθαι γὰρ αὐτάρκως τὴν ἐπανόρθωσιν καὶ διὰ τοῦ προτέρου, καθ’ ὃ περὶ τὰς πλευρὰς ὁ βραχίων παρατεταμένος ἐπήγετο, τοῦ ἀγκῶνος αὐτόθι μένοντος, οὐχ ὥσπερ νῦν ἐπὶ τὸ στῆθος παραγενομένου. τοῦτ’ οὖν παρέχειν ἐπὶ τὸ στῆθος οὐχ ὡς ἀχρήστου τοῦ προτέρου φευκτέον ἡμῖν ἐστιν, ἀλλ’ ὡς καὶ διακινουμένου τὰ πολλὰ τῆς προκειμένης ἐνεργείας κατορθοῦσθαι δυναμένης. εἰ δ’ ἐν τῷ πράττειν αὐτὴν μὴ ὑπακούοι τὸ ἔργον, οὕτως ἐπὶ τὸ δεύτερον σχῆμα μεταβησόμεθα. 423

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: διακινουμένου, παρέχειν, ἐπήγετο

422 Such a posture evidently draws the head of the humerus away toward the outer region, so that the part of the clavicle which is joined to the acromion is drawn away together with it; but he says that there is, for the most part, no need of such a posture. For the restoration is accomplished sufficiently even by means of the former one, in which the upper arm, extended along the ribs, was brought up, the elbow remaining where it is and not, as now, being brought across to the chest. Therefore bringing this across to the chest is to be avoided by us, not on the ground that the former posture is useless, but on the ground that, even with it being moved about (διακινουμένου), the operation proposed can for the most part be successfully accomplished. But if, in performing it, the task does not respond, then we shall pass over to the second posture. 423

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: παρέχειν (probably corrupt for παράγειν, 'bringing across'), οὐχ ὡς ἀχρήστου τοῦ προτέρου, διακινουμένου, τὰ πολλὰ, ὑπακούοι τὸ ἔργον, ἐπήγετο

422 Such a posture evidently draws the head of the upper arm away to the outer region, so that the part of the collarbone joined to the acromion is drawn away along with it. But he says that for the most part there is no need of such a posture, for the correction is accomplished sufficiently by the former one as well, in which the upper arm, stretched along the ribs, was brought up while the elbow stayed where it was, and was not brought across to the chest as it is now. So this bringing of the arm to the chest is to be avoided by us, not as though the former posture were useless, but because, with the former also moving the parts, the operation in hand can for the most part be brought to a successful result. But if, when we perform it, the result does not respond, we shall then pass over to the second posture. 423
